## 44. EVALUATION METRICS

| # | Question | Status | Answer (project as built) | Evidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 813 | What does "correct" mean for this system? | ANSWERED | "Correct" means the answer is judged correct by the self‑hosted gpt‑oss‑120b model, i.e., it matches the reference facts (gold anchors and reference answer) used in the evaluation harness. | `scripts/policy/eval_bajaj_answers.py`; `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 6. Evaluation metrics the harness already computes (s.44) |
| 814 | Is answer accuracy enough? | ANSWERED | No – answer accuracy alone is not sufficient; the project also measures explanation completeness, hallucination/unsupported claims, guarantee‑violation rate, abstention, communication adaptation, what‑if correctness, reliability/fallbacks, latency and cost proxies, and retrieval quality. | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 6. Evaluation metrics the harness already computes (s.44) |
| 815 | How do we measure factual correctness? | ANSWERED | Factual correctness is measured with the ungrounded_number_rate for numeric hallucinations and, for policy answers, with citation_support, strict_citation_correct, and citation_faithful_given_answered metrics. | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 6. Evaluation metrics the harness already computes (s.44) |
| 816 | How do we measure evidence grounding? | ANSWERED | Evidence grounding is measured with metrics such as `ungrounded_number_rate` for unsupported numbers, and for policy answers `citation_support`, `strict_citation_correct` and `citation_faithful_given_answered` which assess whether citations back the answer and are faithful. | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 6. Evaluation metrics the harness already computes (s.44) |
| 817 | How do we measure policy grounding? | ANSWERED | Policy grounding is measured with citation‑based metrics: citation support (at least one cited passage is from a gold document and contains the gold anchor), strict citation correctness (all cited passages are from gold documents), and citation faithfulness (every claim in the answer is supported by its citations). | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 6. Evaluation metrics the harness already computes (s.44) |
| 818 | How do we measure explanation faithfulness? | ANSWERED | Explanation faithfulness is measured both deterministically (by checking reason/value coverage, ungrounded numbers, and gold‑passage citation support and strictness) and with an LLM judge that verifies that every claim is supported by the cited passages. | `docs/research/xai-and-reason-codes.md` § 2. How this applies to the project (decisions already evident in the code); `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 6. Evaluation metrics the harness already computes (s.44) |
| 819 | How do we measure explanation completeness? | ANSWERED | Explanation completeness is measured by the `reason_value_coverage` (and `reason_value_coverage_all`) metric, which verifies that every failed ZEN reason and its value appear in the explanation (A1). | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 6. Evaluation metrics the harness already computes (s.44) |
| 820 | How do we measure explanation relevance? | ANSWERED | Explanation relevance is measured by the reason‑value coverage metrics (`reason_value_coverage` and `reason_value_coverage_all`), which check that every failed ZEN reason and its value appear in the explanation. | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 6. Evaluation metrics the harness already computes (s.44) |
| 821 | How do we measure explanation clarity? | PARTIAL | The project measures aspects of explanation clarity through the “Communication adaptation” metric, which compares readability and jargon differences across literacy levels and adds a judge rating, but there is no dedicated “clarity” metric. | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 6. Evaluation metrics the harness already computes (s.44) |
| 822 | How do we measure actionability? | UNKNOWN | The materials state that actionability is covered under the “Explanation faithfulness / actionability” metric family, using counterfactual explanations and reason‑code rules, but they do not provide a concrete measurement method or metric implementation. **Who must answer:** the project team |  |
| 823 | How do we measure counterfactual correctness? | ANSWERED | Counterfactual correctness is measured by verifying that the what‑if simulation produces the same result as a direct ZEN run on the altered inputs and that the original record remains unchanged. | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 6. Evaluation metrics the harness already computes (s.44) |
| 824 | How do we measure reapplication guidance correctness? | UNKNOWN | The provided materials do not specify a metric for measuring the correctness of reapplication guidance; the team must define this metric. **Who must answer:** VaaniLabs / the project team |  |
| 825 | How do we measure refusal correctness? | PARTIAL | The project measures refusal correctness indirectly through the evaluation harness metrics: refusals are treated as abstentions and are evaluated via the judged answer‑correctness and abstention rates (answered_rate and unanswerable‑abstention counts) in the policy‑split and agent‑eval runs. | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 6. Evaluation metrics the harness already computes (s.44) |
| 826 | How do we measure escalation correctness? | UNKNOWN | The provided materials do not specify how escalation correctness is measured; this must be defined by the project team or VaaniLabs. **Who must answer:** VaaniLabs / the project team |  |
| 827 | How do we measure hallucination rate? | ANSWERED | Hallucination is measured using the ungrounded number rate (numbers not in the facts) together with citation‑based metrics like citation support, strict citation correctness, and citation faithfulness. | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 6. Evaluation metrics the harness already computes (s.44) |
| 828 | How do we measure unsupported-claim rate? | ANSWERED | The project measures unsupported‑claim (hallucination) rate using the `ungrounded_number_rate` metric for numbers not found in the facts, and citation‑based metrics such as `citation_support`, `strict_citation_correct`, and `citation_faithful_given_answered` that check whether atomic facts are backed by reliable sources. | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 6. Evaluation metrics the harness already computes (s.44); `docs/research/regulation-and-design-references.md` § 3. Published metric families for the evaluation questions (s.44, s.45) |
| 829 | How do we measure guarantee-violation rate? | ANSWERED | Guarantee‑violation rate is measured by counting deterministic guarantee language flags in final answers (the `answer_flags`/`deterministic_guarantee_or_decision_language_in_final_answers` metric) and adding the judge‑reported violation rate (`judge_violation_rate`). | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 6. Evaluation metrics the harness already computes (s.44); `scripts/agent/eval_agent.py` |
| 830 | How do we measure memory across multiple turns? | UNKNOWN | The provided materials describe how conversation memory is stored (e.g., a LangGraph MemorySaver keyed by applicant and conversation ID) but do not specify any metric or method for measuring memory usage across multiple turns. **Who must answer:** VaaniLabs / the project team |  |
| 831 | How do we measure communication adaptation? | ANSWERED | Communication adaptation is measured using A2 literacy pairs (low vs high), comparing readability/jargon differences and a judge’s rating of the paired explanations. | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 6. Evaluation metrics the harness already computes (s.44); `docs/research/regulation-and-design-references.md` § Fairness of explanations (s.41) |
| 832 | How do we measure applicant comprehension? | UNKNOWN | The project includes a metric for communication adaptation – A2 literacy pairs compare low vs high literacy answers using readability/jargon differences and a judge rating – but it does not provide a dedicated measure of applicant comprehension. **Who must answer:** the project team |  |
| 833 | How do we measure user satisfaction? | UNKNOWN | The provided materials list many system metrics (e.g., explanation completeness, latency, citation support) but do not specify any measure of user satisfaction; this requires input from real users or the sponsor. **Who must answer:** VaaniLabs / the project team |  |
| 834 | How do we measure latency? | ANSWERED | Latency is measured by recording the response time in milliseconds for each operation and reporting the 50th‑percentile (p50) and 95th‑percentile (p95) values (e.g. `latency_ms_p50` and `latency_ms_p95`). The evaluation harness extracts these from the `ms` fields in the test results and from retrieval metadata. | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 6. Evaluation metrics the harness already computes (s.44); `scripts/agent/eval_agent.py` |
| 835 | How do we measure cost? | ANSWERED | Cost is measured using latency and cost proxy metrics such as p50/p95 latency, time‑to‑first‑token, prompt and completion token counts, tokens per second, and retrieval latency. | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 6. Evaluation metrics the harness already computes (s.44) |
| 836 | How do we measure tool-call efficiency? | PARTIAL | The project measures tool‑call efficiency via the latency and cost proxies collected by the evaluation harness (e.g., p50/p95 latency, TTFT, token counts) and by tracing each tool call as a TOOL span in OpenTelemetry, which records timings and result attributes. However, no dedicated “tool‑call efficiency” metric is defined beyond these proxies. | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 6. Evaluation metrics the harness already computes (s.44); `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/agent/observability.py` |
| 837 | How do we measure retrieval quality? | ANSWERED | Retrieval quality is measured with IR metrics such as Recall@k, MRR, nDCG, citation precision, and latency, reported in the RAG evaluation JSON reports. | handoff: loan_advisory_agent_comprehensive_handoff.md § Phase 4 --- RAG implementation and evaluation; `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 6. Evaluation metrics the harness already computes (s.44); PR #4 (feat/policy-register-rag): Phase 4: policy register + hybrid RAG (EmbeddingGemma 2 + Qdrant + BM25) with 96-question eval § **Phase 4: policy source register + hybrid RAG evaluation** (stacked 4/4, on #3). Retrieval only; no answer generation, no agent.; run report: out/loan-bajaj/RESULTS.md § Retrieval, held-out test, answerable n = 80 |
| 838 | How do we measure system reliability? | ANSWERED | System reliability is measured by the evaluation harness’s `fallback_template_rate` metric, which records how often the system falls back to a template (i.e., a reliability‑related fallback). | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 6. Evaluation metrics the harness already computes (s.44) |
| 839 | What should the pass threshold be for each metric? | UNKNOWN | The materials do not specify any pass thresholds for the evaluation metrics; the project team must define them. **Who must answer:** the project team |  |

## 45. BASELINES

| # | Question | Status | Answer (project as built) | Evidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 840 | What should we compare our system against? | ANSWERED | We should compare our system against (a) the conventional workflow with no LLM (ZEN decision + deterministic templates) and (b) retrieval variants (dense, hybrid, and reranked) using paired bootstrap RAG evaluation reports. | `docs/research/regulation-and-design-references.md` § Baselines (s.45) |
| 841 | Should the baseline be a static FAQ? | UNKNOWN | The project does not decide whether a static FAQ should be used as a baseline; this is a product decision that VaaniLabs must answer. **Who must answer:** VaaniLabs |  |
| 842 | Should the baseline be a traditional support workflow? | UNKNOWN | The materials do not state whether the baseline should be a traditional support workflow; they note a static FAQ or human‑support baseline was not run and that defining a meaningful improvement is a product decision for VaaniLabs. **Who must answer:** VaaniLabs |  |
| 843 | Should the baseline be a standard RAG chatbot? | UNKNOWN | The project does not decide whether the baseline should be a standard RAG chatbot; it only compares against a conventional workflow and retrieval variants, leaving the baseline choice as a product decision. **Who must answer:** VaaniLabs |  |
| 844 | Should the baseline be an LLM without tools? | ANSWERED | No – the baseline is the conventional workflow with no LLM (ZEN decision + deterministic templates), not an LLM without tools. | `docs/research/regulation-and-design-references.md` § Baselines (s.45) |
| 845 | Should the baseline be an LLM with policy retrieval? | UNKNOWN | The materials do not state whether the baseline should be an LLM with policy retrieval; this is left as a product decision for VaaniLabs. **Who must answer:** VaaniLabs |  |
| 846 | Should the baseline use structured rules without an LLM? | ANSWERED | Yes – the baseline is the conventional workflow that relies on deterministic ZEN rules and templates, i.e., structured rules without any LLM. | `docs/research/regulation-and-design-references.md` § Baselines (s.45) |
| 847 | What does a meaningful improvement over baseline look like? | UNKNOWN | The project does not define what a meaningful improvement over baseline looks like; it states this is a product decision that VaaniLabs must answer. **Who must answer:** VaaniLabs |  |

## 46. OBSERVABILITY

| # | Question | Status | Answer (project as built) | Evidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 848 | What should be observable for every conversation? | ANSWERED | Every conversation should be observable via OpenTelemetry spans that capture the turn and graph (`advisor.turn`, `node.<name>`, `tool.<name>`), backend calls (e.g. `frappe.get_loan_application`, `zen.simulate`), LLM calls (`llm.<tag>` with token and latency data) and voice processing events. The self-hosted Phoenix exporter receives them when `AGENT_TRACING=1`; the optional self-hosted Langfuse exporter (`LANGFUSE_ENABLED=1`, off by default) receives the same span tree through the same `TracerProvider`. | `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/agent/observability.py` § module docstring; `docs/observability/langfuse.md` § What is traced |
| 849 | Should every tool call be logged? | ANSWERED | Yes – the design logs every tool call as a TOOL span in the OpenTelemetry trace. | `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/agent/observability.py`; `docs/agent/README.md` § Observability; `tests/test_agent_graph.py` |
| 850 | Should every retrieval step be visible? | ANSWERED | Yes – the design makes every retrieval step visible by tracing it as a TOOL span and recording its attributes. | `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/agent/observability.py`; `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/agent/tools.py` |
| 851 | Should every policy lookup be visible? | UNKNOWN | The materials do not state whether every policy lookup should be visible; this decision must be made by the lender deploying the system (and VaaniLabs as its provider). **Who must answer:** VaaniLabs / a partner lender |  |
| 852 | Should every calculation be logged? | UNKNOWN | The materials do not state whether every calculation must be logged; this decision is left to the lender deploying the system (and VaaniLabs as provider). **Who must answer:** VaaniLabs / a partner lender |  |
| 853 | Should every model call be logged? | UNKNOWN | Not answered by the project materials. **Who must answer:** the project team |  |
| 854 | Should every response validation result be logged? | UNKNOWN | The materials do not state whether every response validation result must be logged; they only show that errors and trace availability are logged. **Who must answer:** the project team |  |
| 855 | Should evidence references be logged? | ANSWERED | Yes – the system records evidence references in its audit logs; the _audit method stores a row with an "evidence_refs" field. | `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/agent/graph.py` |
| 856 | Should agent decisions be traceable? | ANSWERED | Yes - the system makes agent decisions traceable with OpenTelemetry spans for each turn, node and tool. The self-hosted Arize Phoenix exporter is enabled with `AGENT_TRACING=1`; an optional self-hosted Langfuse exporter can receive the same spans and is off by default, so it is neither a runtime dependency nor in the request path. Neither exporter includes message or answer text without its own content flag. | `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/agent/observability.py`; `docs/observability/langfuse.md` § Architecture and data flow |
| 857 | What metrics should appear on the observability dashboard? | PARTIAL | The dashboard should surface the OpenTelemetry spans - one CHAIN span per turn plus node and tool spans - with LLM metrics such as prompt/completion/total tokens, time-to-first-token and overall latency, and the evaluation metrics the harness already computes (explanation completeness, hallucination rate, guarantee-violation rate, abstention, latency percentiles, retrieval quality). Phoenix shows LLM/TOOL/CHAIN views; the optional Langfuse UI adds trace, latency/token and evaluation views over the same spans. | `docs/observability/langfuse.md` § What is traced, § Evaluation workflow; `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 6. Evaluation metrics the harness already computes (s.44); `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/agent/observability.py` |
| 858 | How should latency be broken down? | ANSWERED | Latency should be broken down by component: overall turn latency (p50/p95), LLM latency (including TTFT and prompt/completion token counts), retrieval latency, and any tool/backend call latencies. | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 6. Evaluation metrics the harness already computes (s.44) |
| 859 | How should cost be broken down? | PARTIAL | The materials list some cost components (image size, idle RAM, volumes, and build‑cache size) but do not provide a full, itemised cost breakdown for the system. | `docs/prototypes/p1-resource-usage.md` § Assessment; handoff: loan_advisory_agent_comprehensive_handoff.md § Cost/infra |
| 860 | How should accuracy be represented? | PARTIAL | The project reports accuracy as a numeric "test_accuracy" value (e.g., 0.957) in its evaluation JSON reports, alongside other metrics. | `docs/policy/rag_eval_v1_report.json` |
| 861 | How should problem-specific metrics be represented? | ANSWERED | Problem‑specific metrics should be emitted as a structured dictionary (JSON) where each metric name maps to its numeric value (e.g., mean‑rounded) and an accompanying count “n”. The harness builds nested dicts (overall, by_category, by_origin, latency_ms, etc.) that follow this pattern. | `scripts/policy/eval_rag.py` |
| 862 | Should failed requests be inspectable step by step? | ANSWERED | Yes – the project records failed requests in OpenTelemetry spans, capturing the exception and marking the span with an error status, so each step of a failed request can be inspected via the trace. | `docs/prototypes/p0-zen.md` § Limitations; `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/agent/observability.py` |
| 863 | How do we make debugging possible without exposing sensitive applicant information? | ANSWERED | Debugging is enabled via OpenTelemetry tracing: `AGENT_TRACING=1` enables the Phoenix exporter and `LANGFUSE_ENABLED=1` enables the optional Langfuse exporter. Spans record IDs, intents, flags, token counts and timings but omit message and answer text unless the matching content flag is set - `AGENT_TRACE_CONTENT=1` for Phoenix or `LANGFUSE_CAPTURE_CONTENT=1` for Langfuse. The flags are independent and off by default, and the Langfuse processor masks `input.value`, `output.value`, `llm.system` and `llm.error` at export. | `docs/observability/langfuse.md` § Privacy boundary; `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/agent/observability.py`; `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/agent/langfuse_config.py`; `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 3. Privacy (s.20) and data security |

## 47. SYSTEM FAILURE

| # | Question | Status | Answer (project as built) | Evidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 864 | What happens when the LLM fails? | ANSWERED | If the LLM raises an error, the system falls back to a failure text and returns a draft of None with guard flags indicating a deadline or generic LLM error, ensuring the turn completes safely. | `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/agent/graph.py`; `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/agent/streaming.py` |
| 865 | What happens when the database is unavailable? | ANSWERED | If the database (and thus the Frappe loan system) is unavailable, the system treats the loan system as unreachable, returns an `unavailable` template with `action="handoff"`, and provides `ERROR` evidence instead of a decision. | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 2. Human handoff (s.22); `docs/prototypes/p1-live-integration.md` § Live round trip (no P0 logic changed) |
| 866 | What happens when policy retrieval fails? | ANSWERED | When policy retrieval fails, the system falls back safely: it returns no passages and an error metadata entry, and the agent abstains rather than guessing. | `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/agent/graph.py` |
| 867 | What happens when applicant retrieval fails? | ANSWERED | If applicant retrieval fails, the system stops and returns an “unavailable” handoff response, informing the user it cannot reach the loan system and will not guess the decision, optionally offering to pass the query to a human reviewer. | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 2. Human handoff (s.22); handoff: loan_advisory_agent_comprehensive_handoff.md § Routing |
| 868 | What happens when calculations fail? | ANSWERED | If a calculation (e.g., validation, ZEN engine, or any adapter step) fails, the system returns an ERROR evidence object and never fabricates a decision. | `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/application/decision_orchestrator.py`; `docs/prototypes/project-status.md` § B. What is actually implemented |
| 869 | What happens when the validation layer fails? | ANSWERED | If the validation layer fails, the orchestrator returns an ERROR evidence object and does not fabricate a decision. | `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/application/decision_orchestrator.py` |
| 870 | What happens when the system times out? | ANSWERED | When a turn exceeds its deadline the LLM stream is aborted, a filler or busy template is emitted, and the system falls back to a localized "busy, ask again in a moment" response. | `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/agent/streaming.py`; PR #8 (feat/agent-upgrades): Agent upgrades: guarded sentence streaming for voice, per-turn deadline + fallback, citation verification (opt-in), /voice contrast § What changes; `tests/test_agent_upgrades.py` |
| 871 | What happens when a tool returns malformed data? | ANSWERED | If a tool returns malformed or otherwise invalid data, the system treats it as a failure and returns `ERROR` evidence; the exception is recorded in the tracing span and no decision is fabricated. | `docs/prototypes/p1-live-integration.md` § Live round trip (no P0 logic changed); `tests/test_agent_graph.py` |
| 872 | What happens when the agent enters an incorrect reasoning path? | PARTIAL | If the agent follows a wrong reasoning path, the system’s fail‑safe mechanisms treat it as a failure and return an error/fallback response (e.g., an ERROR evidence object or a generic internal‑error message) instead of fabricating a decision. | `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/application/decision_orchestrator.py`; `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/agent/server.py` |
| 873 | What happens when multiple components disagree? | ANSWERED | If components disagree the orchestrator treats it as a failure and returns an ERROR evidence object, never fabricating a decision. | `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/application/decision_orchestrator.py`; `APPROACH.md` § Approach |
| 874 | Should the system fail closed or fail open? | ANSWERED | The system is designed to fail closed; any tampering with the policy registry or mismatched active version triggers a closed‑failure error. | `decision_engine/policies/registry.json`; `docs/prototypes/p1-p0-comparison.md` § P1 vs P0 Comparison |
| 875 | What message should the applicant receive when the system cannot safely answer? | ANSWERED | The applicant should see the ‘unavailable’ template: “I can’t reach the loan system right now, so I won’t guess about your decision … or I can pass your question to a human reviewer.” | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 2. Human handoff (s.22) |
| 876 | When should failure automatically trigger human handoff? | ANSWERED | The system automatically hands off to a human whenever a failure occurs such as a data dispute, the loan system (Frappe) being unreachable, the application being in manual review, or an override/record‑edit request that is refused. | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 2. Human handoff (s.22) |

## 48. PROMPT ENGINEERING

| # | Question | Status | Answer (project as built) | Evidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 877 | What prompts does the system require? | PARTIAL | The system requires an explain‑intent prompt (identified as "compose_explain") that is fed a system prompt containing channel, locale and literacy cues, and a policy‑compose prompt ("compose_policy") for citation‑mode answers. The explain prompt is retried and may be invoked twice, and the system prompt must include markers like “SPOKEN” and the language name (e.g., “Telugu”). | `tests/test_agent_graph.py` |
| 878 | Which prompts are system-level? | UNKNOWN | The materials do not specify which prompts are classified as system-level; this information must be provided by the project team. **Who must answer:** VaaniLabs / the project team |  |
| 879 | Which prompts are task-specific? | ANSWERED | The project uses distinct, task‑specific prompts identified by their tag: the "compose_explain" prompt is used for the explain intent, the "compose_policy" prompt for policy generation, and separate verification prompts such as VERIFY_CITATIONS and VERIFY_CITATIONS_QUOTE for citation checking. | `tests/test_agent_graph.py`; `tests/test_agent_upgrades.py`; `tests/test_citations.py` |
| 880 | Which prompts are used for question classification? | UNKNOWN | The materials do not specify which prompts are used for question classification; they only mention classification settings (e.g., budget and fast_classify) but no prompt definitions. **Who must answer:** the project team |  |
| 881 | Which prompts are used for explanation generation? | ANSWERED | Explanation generation uses the `compose_explain` prompt (the system prompt for the explain intent), with variations that incorporate literacy, voice/channel, locale, and guarantee handling. | `tests/test_agent_graph.py`; `tests/test_agent_upgrades.py` |
| 882 | Which prompts are used for education? | UNKNOWN | Not answered by the project materials. **Who must answer:** VaaniLabs / the project team |  |
| 883 | Which prompts are used for counterfactual reasoning? | UNKNOWN | The materials do not specify which prompts are used for counterfactual reasoning; the project defines no such prompt templates. **Who must answer:** VaaniLabs / the project team |  |
| 884 | Which prompts are used for response validation? | UNKNOWN | Not answered by the project materials. **Who must answer:** the project team |  |
| 885 | Which prompts are used for refusal? | ANSWERED | Refusal prompts are defined by the guard‑to‑template mapping, e.g. the `guarantee` guard uses the "I can't promise or predict approval ..." template, and similar guards (`override`, `misrepresentation`, `not_available`, `injection`, `auto_submit`, `edit_record`, `dispute`) each have their own fixed refusal template. | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 4. Safety edge cases (s.35); `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 1. Data correction and disputes (s.19) |
| 886 | Which prompts are used for human handoff? | ANSWERED | The system uses fixed handoff templates – the “unavailable” template, the “manual_review” template, the “dispute” template and the override/record‑edit refusal template – each of which returns an `action="handoff"` response. | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 2. Human handoff (s.22) |
| 887 | How will prompts be versioned? | ANSWERED | Prompts will be versioned by placing them in a dedicated prompt pack directory (`prompts/`) that includes versioned prompt files. | `docs/architecture/repository-structure.md` § Future placement |
| 888 | How will prompt changes be evaluated? | ANSWERED | Prompt changes are validated with automated tests that inspect the generated LLM prompts and guard flags (e.g., checking that literacy or locale changes appear in the system prompt and that guarantee language is filtered), and further evaluated by running the agent on the benchmark suite with the evaluation harness (eval_agent.py, eval_citation_verify.py) to measure metrics such as citation correctness, recall@k, and throughput. | `tests/test_agent_graph.py`; run report: out/loan-finish/RESULTS.md § What was tried (selected on DEV; TEST run after the choice) |
| 889 | How do we prevent prompt drift? | ANSWERED | Prompt drift is prevented by keeping all underwriting logic out of prompts – rules live only in the deterministic ZEN decision models, and the agent uses guard templates and tests to enforce consistent prompt content. | `APPROACH.md` § Approach |
| 890 | How do we test prompts against hallucination? | ANSWERED | The project tests prompts for hallucination by injecting known false numbers or guarantees into scripted LLM prompts and asserting that guard flags catch them, and by measuring the `ungrounded_number_rate` metric during evaluation. | `tests/test_agent_graph.py`; `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 6. Evaluation metrics the harness already computes (s.44) |
| 891 | How do we test prompts against prompt injection? | UNKNOWN | Not answered by the project materials. **Who must answer:** the project team |  |
| 892 | How do we ensure prompts do not cause policy invention? | ANSWERED | We prevent policy invention by keeping all underwriting rules exclusively in the ZEN decision models and never embedding them in prompts; guards also detect and block invented numbers or injection attempts. | `APPROACH.md` § Approach; `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 4. Safety edge cases (s.35) |

## 49. MODEL SELECTION

| # | Question | Status | Answer (project as built) | Evidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 893 | What model capability is actually required? | ANSWERED | Only a deterministic rule‑simulation capability (the ZEN engine) is required; no ML risk model is needed for the MVP. | `APPROACH.md` § Approach; handoff: loan_advisory_agent_comprehensive_handoff.md § Architecture risks |
| 894 | Do we need a large reasoning model? | UNKNOWN | The materials do not state whether a large reasoning model is required; this must be answered by the project team or VaaniLabs. **Who must answer:** VaaniLabs / the project team |  |
| 895 | Do we need a smaller model for classification? | UNKNOWN | The project notes classification latency problems and suggests a shorter classify path (e.g., regex first) rather than explicitly requiring a smaller model, so the need for a smaller model is not definitively answered. **Who must answer:** the project team |  |
| 896 | Do we need separate models for different tasks? | ANSWERED | Yes – the project uses separate models: a deterministic ZEN decision model for underwriting and a distinct LLM‑based agent for explanations, keeping the tasks isolated. | `APPROACH.md` § Approach |
| 897 | What model provides acceptable quality? | ANSWERED | The hybrid retriever (weighted RRf) model – configured with k = 10, w_dense = 1.0, w_bm25 = 0.5 – provides acceptable quality, achieving the best dev MRR@10 of 0.628. | `config/rag.yaml` |
| 898 | What model provides acceptable latency? | UNKNOWN | The project records latency for the gpt‑oss‑120b (vLLM) model (e.g., "latency_s" and model name are logged), but it does not explicitly state that this model meets an acceptable latency threshold. **Who must answer:** the project team |  |
| 899 | What model provides acceptable cost? | UNKNOWN | The system uses the BAAI/bge‑reranker‑v2‑m3 CrossEncoder reranker model (see the CrossEncoderReranker class), but the materials do not state whether its cost is acceptable. **Who must answer:** the project team |  |
| 900 | Can the system use open-source models? | ANSWERED | Yes. The system is built to load and run open‑source models such as Google’s EmbeddingGemma‑2 for dense retrieval and BAAI’s bge‑reranker‑v2‑m3 for reranking. | `scripts/policy/eval_rag.py`; `rag/retrieve.py` |
| 901 | Can the system be deployed with self-hosted models? | ANSWERED | Yes – the system is designed to run with a self‑hosted LLM (e.g., gpt‑oss‑120b via vLLM) as shown by its OpenAI‑compatible client pointing to a local endpoint. | `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/agent/llm.py` |
| 902 | What are the trade-offs between hosted and self-hosted models? | UNKNOWN | The provided materials do not discuss the trade‑offs between hosted and self‑hosted models. **Who must answer:** VaaniLabs / the project team |  |
| 903 | What happens if the chosen model becomes unavailable? | PARTIAL | The system includes retry logic in the LLM client and a "fallback_template" option in the graph, so if a model is unavailable it will retry and can fall back to a template, though the exact behavior isn’t fully detailed. | `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/agent/graph.py`; `scripts/data/narrate_personas.py` |
| 904 | How dependent is the architecture on one model provider? | ANSWERED | The architecture is not tied to a single model provider; the LLM endpoint and model name are configurable via environment variables, allowing different self‑hosted or external models to be swapped in. | `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/agent/llm.py` |

## 50. TOOL DESIGN

| # | Question | Status | Answer (project as built) | Evidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 905 | What tools should the agent have? | ANSWERED | The agent should expose only a set of least‑privilege, typed tools: authorize, get_application_summary, get_decision_evidence, run_what_if, improvement_plan, search_approved_policy, and create_human_handoff, with no generic Frappe/SQL or write‑path tools. | PR #6 (feat/langgraph-agent): Phase 5: single LangGraph advisory agent over ZEN evidence, Frappe and policy RAG (explains, never decides) § What is in it; `docs/agent/README.md` § Tools (`tools.py`, the only things the graph can touch) |
| 906 | Should it have an applicant lookup tool? | ANSWERED | No – the design deliberately excludes a generic applicant‑lookup tool, providing only specific least‑privilege tools such as `authorize`, `get_application_summary` and `get_decision_evidence`. | `docs/agent/README.md` § Tools (`tools.py`, the only things the graph can touch); run report: out/loan-agent/RESULTS.md § 2. Architecture (from `docs/agent/README.md`) |
| 907 | Should it have a decision lookup tool? | ANSWERED | The project deliberately does not include a generic decision lookup tool; instead it provides the read‑only `get_decision_evidence` tool to retrieve stored decision evidence, and no other decision‑lookup capability is offered. | `docs/agent/README.md` § Tools (`tools.py`, the only things the graph can touch); `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/agent/tools.py` |
| 908 | Should it have a policy search tool? | ANSWERED | Yes – the design explicitly includes a policy search tool (`search_approved_policy`) to retrieve approved policy passages with lender‑profile filters. | handoff: loan_advisory_agent_comprehensive_handoff.md § Proposed initial tools; `docs/agent/README.md` § Tools (`tools.py`, the only things the graph can touch) |
| 909 | Should it have an eligibility calculator? | UNKNOWN | The project materials do not state whether an eligibility calculator should be included; this design decision is not addressed. **Who must answer:** VaaniLabs / the project team |  |
| 910 | Should it have a counterfactual simulator? | UNKNOWN | Not answered by the project materials. **Who must answer:** the project team |  |
| 911 | Should it have a disclosure checker? | UNKNOWN | The materials do not state whether a disclosure checker tool should be included; the decision is not addressed. **Who must answer:** the project team |  |
| 912 | Should it have a conversation-state tool? | UNKNOWN | The materials describe conversation state handling but do not state a decision on adding a dedicated conversation‑state tool. **Who must answer:** VaaniLabs / the project team |  |
| 913 | Should it have a human-handoff tool? | ANSWERED | Yes – the project deliberately includes a human‑handoff tool. The design defines handoff triggers and implements a `create_human_handoff` tool that logs a handoff record and returns a reference. | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 2. Human handoff (s.22); `data/sources/policy_register.yaml` |
| 914 | What inputs should each tool accept? | UNKNOWN | Not answered by the project materials. **Who must answer:** the project team |  |
| 915 | What outputs should each tool return? | ANSWERED | Each tool must return a typed response following the output contract: a dict containing `request_id`, `answer`, `action` (one of answer \| clarify \| simulate \| handoff \| refuse), `citations[]`, `evidence_refs[]`, `missing_information[]`, optional `handoff_ref`, `trace_id`, and any structured status or error fields. The tool decorator also records result fields such as `simulated_decision`, `original_record_unchanged`, `decision`, and `record_id`. | handoff: loan_advisory_agent_comprehensive_handoff.md § Output contract (proposal); `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/agent/observability.py` |
| 916 | Which tools should be callable by the model? | ANSWERED | The model may only call the explicitly defined least‑privilege tools in tools.py: `authorize`, `get_application_summary`, `get_decision_evidence`, `run_what_if`, `improvement_plan`, `search_approved_policy`, and `create_human_handoff`. No generic Frappe, SQL, web, or write‑path tools are exposed. | `docs/agent/README.md` § Tools (`tools.py`, the only things the graph can touch); `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/agent/tools.py` |
| 917 | Which tools should only be callable by deterministic code? | ANSWERED | The deterministic code should be the only caller of the least‑privilege tools: `authorize`, `get_application_summary`, `get_decision_evidence`, `run_what_if`, `improvement_plan`, `search_approved_policy`, and `create_human_handoff`. | PR #6 (feat/langgraph-agent): Phase 5: single LangGraph advisory agent over ZEN evidence, Frappe and policy RAG (explains, never decides) § What is in it; `docs/agent/README.md` § Tools (`tools.py`, the only things the graph can touch) |
| 918 | How should tool results be validated? | ANSWERED | Tool results must be validated by mapping raw data to a canonical pydantic schema and catching any validation, unknown‑application, or ZEN engine errors; on failure the system returns an ERROR evidence object rather than fabricating a decision. | `README.md` § Loan Advisory Agent — P0 (decision core) + P1 (live Frappe); `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/application/decision_orchestrator.py`; `docs/prototypes/p0-integration.md` § P0 Integration |
| 919 | What happens when a tool returns conflicting data? | ANSWERED | When a tool returns conflicting data, the system keeps both values and surfaces the conflict to the user rather than arbitrarily picking one. | `docs/policy/bajaj_reference_lender.md` § 2.3 Conflict and staleness register (Bajaj sources) |

| # | Question | Status | Answer (project as built) | Evidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 961 | What needs to be publicly accessible? | UNKNOWN | The materials do not specify which components must be publicly accessible; the team or VaaniLabs need to define the required public endpoints. **Who must answer:** VaaniLabs / the project team |  |
| 962 | What information can be safely exposed in the public demo? | ANSWERED | The public demo may expose only synthetic demo applicant records, their decision cards and the synthetic‑policy disclaimer – all marked as synthetic and containing no real personal data. | `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/agent/server.py`; `apps/web/chat/index.html` |
| 963 | How should demo applicant data be isolated? | ANSWERED | Demo applicant data is kept separate by storing it only in the synthetic data folder and the server’s hard‑coded DEMO list; the sign‑in endpoint only accepts those synthetic refs, ensuring it never mixes with real applicant data. | `README.md` § Where things live; `docs/architecture/repository-structure.md` § Layout; `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/agent/server.py` |
| 964 | How should secrets be protected? | ANSWERED | Secrets must never be committed to the repository; they are kept in untracked environment files (e.g., secrets.env) with restrictive permissions (chmod 600) and accessed via environment variables at runtime. | `README.md` § Live Frappe (P1); `infra/frappe/bootstrap/create_site.sh`; handoff: loan_advisory_agent_comprehensive_handoff.md § P1 --- live integration |
| 965 | How should public access be authenticated? | ANSWERED | Public access must be authenticated with token‑auth using per‑user API keys and secrets in the Authorization header (e.g., `Authorization: token <api_key>:<api_secret>`). | `docs/research/frappe-schema-discovery.md` § 8. APIs (verified); `docs/prototypes/p1-frappe-api.md` § P1 Frappe API (all calls executed against `http://127.0.0.1:8080`); `docs/prototypes/p0-frappe.md` § API (local dev auth = API key + secret) |
| 966 | What should happen if someone enters arbitrary applicant information? | ANSWERED | If arbitrary (missing, invalid or implausible) applicant information is entered, the input is validated by the Pydantic schema which raises errors for out‑of‑range values, and the orchestration layer returns an ERROR decision and transitions the application to the INFO_REQUESTED state (or MANUAL_REVIEW for contradictory data), without persisting a fabricated decision. | `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/application/lifecycle.py`; `decision_engine/schemas/underwriting.py` |
| 967 | How should the demo prevent abuse? | ANSWERED | The demo prevents abuse by restricting access to synthetic accounts only and issuing HMAC‑signed session tokens that encode the applicant and application IDs with an expiry; the server extracts identity from the token (not the request body) and rejects any unsigned or expired token. In production this demo sign‑in must be replaced by real authentication. | `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/agent/server.py` |
| 968 | Can an evaluator inspect the system step by step? | ANSWERED | Yes. The system records a ZEN trace of each node’s input, output and performance, and provides a script (inspect_zen_trace.py) that prints this trace so an evaluator can step through the decision process. | `scripts/development/inspect_zen_trace.py`; `docs/prototypes/p0-zen.md` § Limitations |
| 969 | Can an evaluator inspect evidence retrieval? | ANSWERED | Yes – the project provides artefacts that let an evaluator inspect what evidence was retrieved, such as the cached retrieval passages (`policy_dev/retrieval.json`) and trace dumps that include retrieval latency and citations. | run report: out/loan-upgrade/RESULTS.md § Files (this directory); `README.md` § Run |
| 970 | Can an evaluator inspect policy retrieval? | ANSWERED | Yes. The system provides a `search_approved_policy` tool and a `retrieve_policy` graph method that return the retrieved passages and detailed metadata (mode, lender, latency, etc.), allowing an evaluator to inspect policy retrieval results. | `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/agent/tools.py`; `docs/policy/policy_register_and_rag.md` § 2. Retrieval design (`rag/`) |
| 971 | Can an evaluator inspect agent decisions? | ANSWERED | Yes. The system records an audit log for each turn and provides scripts to dump the ZEN decision trace, allowing an evaluator to inspect the agent’s decisions. | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 2. Human handoff (s.22); `scripts/development/inspect_zen_trace.py` |
| 972 | Can an evaluator inspect latency and cost? | ANSWERED | Yes – the evaluation harness records latency and cost proxy metrics (e.g., latency_ms_p50/p95, ttft, token counts) that an evaluator can inspect. | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 6. Evaluation metrics the harness already computes (s.44); `scripts/agent/eval_agent.py` |

## 56. DATA SECURITY / ARCHITECTURE

| # | Question | Status | Answer (project as built) | Evidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 973 | Where will applicant data be stored? | ANSWERED | Applicant data is stored in the Frappe Lending system of record, i.e., the MariaDB database backing the Frappe stack (persisted in Docker volumes). | `APPROACH.md` § Approach; `docs/prototypes/p1-resource-usage.md` § Volume sizes (measured, `docker system df -v`) |
| 974 | How will applicant data be encrypted? | UNKNOWN | The materials do not specify how applicant data will be encrypted; this decision must be made by the lender deploying the system (and VaaniLabs as its provider). **Who must answer:** VaaniLabs / a partner lender |  |
| 975 | Who can access applicant data? | ANSWERED | Only the authenticated applicant (via their server‑side session) can view their own application data; all other access is blocked or limited to need‑to‑know system components. | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 3. Privacy (s.20) and data security; handoff: loan_advisory_agent_comprehensive_handoff.md § Initial scope decisions; handoff: loan_advisory_agent_comprehensive_handoff.md § Routing |
| 976 | Who can access logs? | UNKNOWN | The materials do not specify which persons or roles are permitted to access the system logs; this must be answered by the project team or the deploying lender. **Who must answer:** a partner lender / the project team |  |
| 977 | What information should be redacted from logs? | UNKNOWN | Not answered by the project materials. **Who must answer:** the project team |  |
| 978 | How will access be controlled? | ANSWERED | Access is controlled by per‑user API token authentication and server‑side session checks; a guard enforces that only the applicant’s own data can be retrieved, never another’s, and all data assets are limited to need‑to‑know. | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 3. Privacy (s.20) and data security; `docs/research/frappe-schema-discovery.md` § 8. APIs (verified) |
| 979 | How will data be isolated between applicants? | ANSWERED | Data is isolated by authorising access server‑side per session and using an `other_applicant` guard that blocks any request to view or discuss another applicant’s details, ensuring each user only sees their own application data. | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 3. Privacy (s.20) and data security |
| 980 | How will session state be protected? | ANSWERED | Session state is protected by an HMAC‑signed session token that encodes the applicant and application references and expiry; authentication and data access are performed server‑side using this token, never from the client payload. | `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/agent/server.py` |
| 981 | How will policy documents be protected? | ANSWERED | Policy documents will be stored in a separate, controlled and versioned document store with a retrieval index, and access to them will be restricted by access‑control mechanisms. | handoff: loan_advisory_agent_comprehensive_handoff.md § State/memory separation; `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 3. Privacy (s.20) and data security |
| 982 | How will backups be handled? | UNKNOWN | The project materials do not specify how backups will be handled; this must be defined by the lender deploying the system (and VaaniLabs as its provider). **Who must answer:** VaaniLabs / a partner lender |  |
| 983 | What happens when a user's data needs to be deleted? | UNKNOWN | The project materials do not specify what actions are taken when a user's data must be deleted; the team must define and implement this behavior. **Who must answer:** the project team |  |

## 58. FINAL SYSTEM BEHAVIOR

| # | Question | Status | Answer (project as built) | Evidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 994 | What should happen when the applicant asks, "Why was my loan rejected?" | ANSWERED | When the applicant asks “Why was my loan rejected?” the system should load the stored Decision Evidence for that application and invoke the agent’s explain branch, returning a grounded, citation‑rich explanation (never a new decision). The response is validated by deterministic guards and falls back to a template if needed. | `docs/agent/README.md` § Phase 5 — Loan Advisory Agent (one LangGraph graph) + Phase 6 voice; run report: out/loan-agent/RESULTS.md § 2. Architecture (from `docs/agent/README.md`) |
| 995 | What should happen when they ask, "What does this rejection reason mean?" | ANSWERED | The agent should retrieve the stored Decision Evidence for the application and explain the specific rejection reason (with any policy citation), never claiming a written reason for NBFCs. | `docs/agent/README.md` § Phase 5 — Loan Advisory Agent (one LangGraph graph) + Phase 6 voice |
| 996 | What should happen when they ask, "What can I change?" | PARTIAL | When the user asks “What can I change?”, the agent should invoke an in‑memory ZEN what‑if simulation to generate concrete improvement suggestions for the applicant’s data, returning grounded advice without altering the stored decision. | PR #6 (feat/langgraph-agent): Phase 5: single LangGraph advisory agent over ZEN evidence, Frappe and policy RAG (explains, never decides) § Phase 5: one LangGraph advisory graph over ZEN evidence, Frappe and the policy RAG; memory: project note (loan-advisory-agent) § Loan Advisory Agent (VaaniLabs.AI capstone; repo Narendarcodes/Load-Advisory-Agent, "Load" spelling) |
| 997 | What should happen when they ask, "How much do I need to change?" | ANSWERED | The agent should give a counterfactual explanation, showing the specific recorded reasons that must change (e.g., credit score, income) to achieve approval, using deterministic decision evidence rather than guessing. | `docs/research/xai-and-reason-codes.md` § Counterfactual explanations; `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/agent/prompts.py` |
| 998 | What should happen when they ask, "What if I clear my credit-card dues?" | ANSWERED | The agent treats the query as a what‑if simulation, applies the reduction in monthly obligations (clearing credit‑card dues), runs a ZEN simulation and returns a “simulate” action with the updated figures and the simulated decision while leaving the original record unchanged. | `tests/test_agent_graph.py` |
| 999 | What should happen when they ask, "What if my salary increases?" | ANSWERED | The agent should treat the query as a what‑if scenario, run the underwriting model with the higher salary, return the updated decision/evidence, and leave the stored application unchanged. | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 1. Data correction and disputes (s.19) |
| 1000 | What should happen when they ask, "Can I apply again now?" | ANSWERED | The system must invoke the `guarantee` guard and refuse to promise approval, responding with the template that says it cannot promise or predict approval and can only offer improvement guidance. | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 4. Safety edge cases (s.35); `tests/e2e/chat_smoke.mjs` § chat_smoke.mjs |
| 1001 | What should happen when they ask, "Will you approve me next time?" | ANSWERED | The agent must refuse to promise or predict future approval, responding with the guarantee guard template (e.g., “I can’t promise or predict approval …”). | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 4. Safety edge cases (s.35) |
| 1002 | What should happen when they ask, "What if I change my job?" | UNKNOWN | The system will treat the query as a what‑if request, classify it under the "what_if" intent and try to extract a change to the employment_months field (the only job‑related variable it knows). It can then run a what‑if simulation, but the materials do not specify a concrete response for a generic “change my job” question, only for changes like staying longer in the current job. **Who must answer:** the project team |  |
| 1003 | What should happen when they ask, "What if I apply for a smaller amount?" | UNKNOWN | Not answered by the project materials. **Who must answer:** the project team |  |
| 1004 | What should happen when they say, "My information is wrong"? | ANSWERED | The system treats the statement as a data dispute, creates a handoff to a human reviewer via HandoffQueue, and replies with a fixed message that it cannot edit the record itself. | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 1. Data correction and disputes (s.19) |
| 1005 | What should happen when they say, "I don't understand"? | UNKNOWN | The provided materials do not describe any specific behavior for the case when a user says “I don’t understand.” **Who must answer:** VaaniLabs / the project team |  |
| 1006 | What should happen when they say, "Explain it simply"? | PARTIAL | The system will treat the utterance as an explain intent and generate a plain‑language answer (using the EXPLAIN prompt). If the user asks for a simple explanation, the agent will apply the low‑literacy style (as shown by the literacy‑style test). | `tests/test_agent_graph.py`; `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/agent/prompts.py` |
| 1007 | What should happen when they say, "Give me the detailed explanation"? | ANSWERED | When the user asks “Give me the detailed explanation,” the agent must return a detailed, evidence‑grounded explanation of the decision, using the stored Decision Evidence, and must not attempt to change or decide the outcome. | `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/agent/prompts.py`; `docs/agent/README.md` § Phase 5 — Loan Advisory Agent (one LangGraph graph) + Phase 6 voice |
| 1008 | What should happen when they ask, "Why was my friend approved?" | UNKNOWN | Not answered by the project materials. **Who must answer:** the project team |  |
| 1009 | What should happen when they ask, "Tell me your exact scoring formula"? | ANSWERED | The agent should reply that the exact scoring formula is not available to it, giving an honest “that isn’t available to me” response. | `tests/test_abstain_unavailable.py` |
| 1010 | What should happen when they ask, "How can I make the system approve me?" | ANSWERED | The system must refuse and respond with the override guard template, e.g., “I can't approve, reverse or change a loan decision …”, without giving any advice on how to get approval. | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 4. Safety edge cases (s.35) |
| 1011 | What should happen when they ask something outside the system's role? | ANSWERED | When a user asks something outside the system’s role, the request is routed to the cite‑or‑abstain policy path; if no valid citation can be provided the agent abstains and returns a localized “service is busy, ask again in a moment” template. | PR #8 (feat/agent-upgrades): Agent upgrades: guarded sentence streaming for voice, per-turn deadline + fallback, citation verification (opt-in), /voice contrast § What changes; run report: out/loan-upgrade/RESULTS.md § 2. Per-turn deadline and graceful fallback (verified) |
| 1012 | What should happen when no reliable answer exists? | ANSWERED | When the system cannot produce a reliable answer it must abstain, never fabricate a response, and reply with a fallback template (e.g., a “Sorry.” or busy message). | `scripts/policy/eval_bajaj_answers.py`; `tests/test_agent_upgrades.py` |
| 1013 | What should happen when the available evidence is insufficient? | ANSWERED | When evidence is insufficient the system returns an ERROR decision evidence entry listing the missing fields and transitions to an INFO_REQUESTED state, prompting clarification or a human handoff. | `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/application/lifecycle.py`; handoff: loan_advisory_agent_comprehensive_handoff.md § Routing |
| 1014 | What should happen when the policy is ambiguous? | ANSWERED | When the policy is ambiguous, the system should surface the conflict (show both versions) rather than choosing a side. | `docs/policy/bajaj_reference_lender.md` § 2.3 Conflict and staleness register (Bajaj sources) |
| 1015 | What should happen when the applicant disputes the decision? | ANSWERED | When an applicant disputes the decision, the system creates a human handoff (data_dispute), returns a response with action="handoff" and a reference ID, and does not modify the application record. | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 1. Data correction and disputes (s.19); `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/agent/graph.py` |
| 1016 | What should happen when the applicant needs human support? | ANSWERED | When an applicant needs human support the system creates a handoff record, returns an API response with `action="handoff"` and a `handoff_ref`, and informs the applicant via a template that a human reviewer will handle the request. | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 2. Human handoff (s.22) |

## 59. CORE DESIGN QUESTIONS

| # | Question | Status | Answer (project as built) | Evidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1017 | What is the single most important job of the agent? | ANSWERED | The agent’s primary purpose is to explain recorded loan decisions; it does not make underwriting decisions itself. | `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/agent/__init__.py` |
| 1018 | What must the agent always know before answering a rejection question? | ANSWERED | The agent must always know the recorded failed rules (the decision evidence) that caused the rejection before answering a rejection question. | `docs/research/xai-and-reason-codes.md` § 2. How this applies to the project (decisions already evident in the code); `docs/research/regulation-and-design-references.md` § Reasons for rejection |
| 1019 | What must the agent never assume? | ANSWERED | The agent must never assume any facts that are not explicitly provided; it must not assume approval, limits, fees, waiting periods, policies, or any other information beyond the given facts. | `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/agent/prompts.py` |
| 1020 | What must the agent never generate without evidence? | ANSWERED | The agent must never generate a decision (or any underwriting conclusion) without evidence; it only explains using stored Decision Evidence. | PR #6 (feat/langgraph-agent): Phase 5: single LangGraph advisory agent over ZEN evidence, Frappe and policy RAG (explains, never decides) § Phase 5: one LangGraph advisory graph over ZEN evidence, Frappe and the policy RAG; `APPROACH.md` § Approach |
| 1021 | What must always come from deterministic logic? | ANSWERED | All underwriting rule evaluations, decision outcomes and related evidence (including request‑guard decisions) must be produced by deterministic logic – i.e., the ZEN engine – and never by prompts, the Frappe layer, or the LLM agent. | `APPROACH.md` § Approach; `docs/agent/README.md` § Graph (`apps/api/src/loan_advisory/agent/graph.py`); `docs/prototypes/p1-test-results.md` § Acceptance (§17) |
| 1022 | What must always come from the lender's policy? | PARTIAL | The limits (i.e., the underwriting thresholds) must always be taken from the lender's policy. | `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/agent/prompts.py` |
| 1023 | What can safely be generated by the LLM? | ANSWERED | The LLM can safely generate the applicant’s own decision record, the rule checks behind it and the values they submitted, while guards block guarantees, overrides, internal confidential info, misrepresentation, and other unsafe content. | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 3. Privacy (s.20) and data security; `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 4. Safety edge cases (s.35) |
| 1024 | What should happen when the evidence and policy do not support an answer? | ANSWERED | When the evidence and policy cannot support an answer, the system must not fabricate a response; it either abstains or escalates to a human, providing clarification and a handoff reference. | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 2. Human handoff (s.22); handoff: loan_advisory_agent_comprehensive_handoff.md § Routing |
| 1025 | What should happen when the applicant asks for something outside the system's authority? | ANSWERED | The system refuses out‑of‑authority requests with a deterministic guard‑template response (e.g., “I can’t edit your application… or pass a correction request to a human reviewer”) and may offer a human handoff. | `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 1. Data correction and disputes (s.19); `docs/research/design-decisions-in-code.md` § 4. Safety edge cases (s.35) |
| 1026 | What is the strongest argument that this system creates value for the applicant? | PARTIAL | The system’s strongest value lies in giving rejected applicants a transparent, rule‑by‑rule explanation plus a concrete improvement plan and safe what‑if simulation, so they can understand the denial and know exactly what to change to become eligible. | `apps/api/src/loan_advisory/agent/prompts.py`; handoff: loan_advisory_agent_comprehensive_handoff.md § Current foundation |
| 1027 | What is the strongest argument that this system creates value for the lender? | UNKNOWN | The provided materials do not contain a concrete argument showing how the system creates value for the lender; that assessment must come from the lender or VaaniLabs. **Who must answer:** VaaniLabs / a partner lender |  |
| 1028 | What is the strongest argument that this system is safer than a generic financial chatbot? | UNKNOWN | Not answered by the project materials. **Who must answer:** the project team |  |
| 1029 | What is the strongest argument that this system is more useful than a static FAQ? | UNKNOWN | The provided materials do not contain a stated argument comparing the system to a static FAQ; VaaniLabs must supply that justification. **Who must answer:** VaaniLabs |  |
| 1030 | What is the strongest argument that this system is more useful than a conventional support agent? | UNKNOWN | The materials do not state the strongest argument for why this system is more useful than a conventional support agent; that assessment is left to a product decision by VaaniLabs. **Who must answer:** VaaniLabs |  |
| 1031 | What is the strongest technical differentiator? | PARTIAL | The system’s key technical edge is its deterministic ZEN underwriting engine, which holds all business rules and produces evidence‑grounded decisions, keeping the LLM out of the core underwriting process. | `APPROACH.md` § Approach; handoff: loan_advisory_agent_comprehensive_handoff.md § Architecture risks |
| 1032 | What is the strongest product differentiator? | UNKNOWN | The materials highlight that the system is an evidence‑grounded advisory agent that explains loan rejections without making decisions, which is presented as a key differentiator, though the claim isn’t explicitly framed as the “strongest” differentiator. **Who must answer:** the project team |  |
| 1033 | What is the strongest evaluation differentiator? | UNKNOWN | The materials do not specify which evaluation metric is the strongest differentiator; the project team must determine this. **Who must answer:** the project team |  |
| 1034 | What would make the solution genuinely meaningful rather than merely "an LLM wrapper"? | UNKNOWN | The project does not specify what would make the solution genuinely meaningful; it states that determining a meaningful improvement is a product decision that VaaniLabs must answer. **Who must answer:** VaaniLabs |  |
| 1035 | What would cause the solution to fail the challenge even if the chatbot looks impressive? | PARTIAL | The solution would fail the challenge if any of the core pipeline safeguards are triggered – for example an unknown application, Frappe being unavailable, validation errors in the canonical schema, or a ZEN node error – which cause the orchestrator to return an "ERROR" decision instead of a valid answer, or if the chatbot tries to embed underwriting rules in prompts (which the design explicitly forbids). | `docs/prototypes/p0-integration.md` § P0 Integration |
| 1036 | What assumptions must be validated before implementation? | ANSWERED | The project must validate that the synthetic underwriting thresholds (income ≥ ₹25k, FOIR/DTI ≤ 45%, credit score ≥ 700, employment ≥ 12 months) are real‑world criteria, that a real primary applicant dataset can be acquired, that an authentic policy source corpus is collected and governed, that the planned RAG retrieval pipeline (Python + Qdrant + hybrid search) works as intended, and that the decision‑evidence contract is finalized before production. | handoff: loan_advisory_agent_comprehensive_handoff.md § P0 --- decision core; handoff: loan_advisory_agent_comprehensive_handoff.md § 5. Current technology decisions at a glance |
| 1037 | What unanswered question could fundamentally change the architecture? | UNKNOWN | The materials do not specify which particular unanswered question could fundamentally change the architecture; the open‑question register is referenced but its contents are not included, so the team must identify the critical open question. **Who must answer:** VaaniLabs / the project team |  |
| 1038 | What unanswered question could fundamentally change the product scope? | UNKNOWN | The materials do not specify a concrete unanswered question that, if resolved, would fundamentally change the product scope; the team must identify such a question. **Who must answer:** VaaniLabs / the project team |  |
| 1039 | What unanswered question could fundamentally change the business case? | UNKNOWN | The materials note several unresolved items (e.g., human sign‑off on the policy register, currency of policy documents, and the lack of real‑lender underwriting rules), but they do not specify which of these could fundamentally alter the business case, so that answer must come from the project team or the lender. **Who must answer:** a partner lender / the project team |  |
| 1040 | What unanswered question could fundamentally change the safety model? | UNKNOWN | The materials do not specify which unanswered question could fundamentally change the safety model; they only note that there is an open‑question register without listing the specific question. **Who must answer:** the project team |  |
